BY EDWARD KENNEDY 


CAIRO, EGYPT, JUNE 1-(AP)-A HIGH RAF SPOKESMAN SAID TODAY THAT 
wees 


GERMANY'S AIR SUPREMACY WHICH SWEPT THE NAZI FORCES THROUGH THE BALK~ 


ANS AND INTO CRETE ENDED AT THAT MEDITERRANEAN ISLAND BECAUSE OF THE 


AIR BASE FACTOR, HE ESTIMATED THE NAZI AIR FORCE THROWN AT CRETE 


AT li, 000 PLANES. 

IF THE GERMANS CARRY THE LUFTWAFFE TO THE MAINLAND OF AFRICA OR 
ASIA MINOR, HE SAID, THE SITUATION WILL BE LARGELY REVERSED AND EVEN 
SHOULD THEY ATTEMPT AIR INVASION OF CYPRUS THEY WILL NOT HAVE THE 
SAME VANTAGE POSITIONS OF BASES AS IN THE ATTACK ON CRETE, 


“THE ESSENCE OF AIR OPERATIONS, AS IN NAVAL OPERATIONS, IS SECURE 


BASES," HE CONTINUED, 
"GERMAN BASES AND THE RANGE THEY HAD EXPLAIN WHY WE COULD NOT 


PUT AN AIR COVER OVER CRETE, AS THE GERMANS en THEY SPREAD 


OUT OBTAINING A SEMI-CIRCLE OF BASES AROUND cage AND WITHIN A SHORT 
DISTANCE OF IT. WE WERE PUSHED BACK ON pete OF THE TRAINGLE," 
IN GREECE, THE OFFICER SAID, THERE WERE MANY FIELDS HOPELESS FOR 
AVIATION IN THE WINTER BUT WHICH BECAME RAPIDLY SUITABLE AFTER THE | 
FIRST FEW DRY DAYS OF SPRING.. 
THIS CHANGE IN GREECE THIS YEAR CAME JUST AS THE GERMANS OCCUPIED 
IT AND CONSEQUENTLY THEY WERE ABLE TO IMPROVE LANDING FIELDS IN MANY 


PLACES WHERE THE BRITISH COULD NOT. 
THE GERMANS" LABOR POLICY ALSO AIDED THEM IN GETTING INNUMERABLE 


FIELDS IN GREECE AND OTHER ISLANDS READY FOR OPERATIONS AGAINST CRETE, 


HE SAID, 


"THEY CONSCRIPTED ALL MALES UP TO 50 AND PUT THEM TO WORK MAKING 
LANDING GROUNDS, 


BUT EVEN WITH OUR GENTLEMANLY METHODS WE COULD 
HAVE IMPROVISED AIRFIELDS A FORTNIGHT LATER, 

DESPITE THE GERMANS’ EXTENSIVE USE OF PARACHUTE TROOPS AND DIVE 
BOMBERS IN CRETE, THIS ‘OFFICIAL ASSERTED, FAR MORE IMPORTANT FACTORS 


WERE THE LANDING OF TROOPS* BY PLANE AND MACHINE-GUNNING ‘FROM LOW AL= 


TITUDES BY FIGHTER PLANES, 
"THE TIDE WAS TURNED BY PLANE,” HE SAID, ADDING IT WAS ATTACKS 


FROM THE AIR RATHER THAN GROUND OPERATIONS BY PARACHUTISTS THAT OPENED 


THE WAY FOR SUCH LANDINGS.

Organization Helped Others — 


How efficacious and suited to the

- 


Nor was this the whole benefit. 
The encyclical Rerum Novaruni, 
coming down to the people and 
greeting them with esteem and 
love, went deep into the hearts 
and esteem of the working class 
and inspired it with a sense of 


Christian sentiment and _ civil 


dignity; indeed, its powerful in- 
fluence came with the passage of 
the years to expand and spread to 
such an extent that its norms be- 
came almost the common property 


of all men, 


And while the State in the Nine- 


| teenth Century, through excessive 


exaltation of liberty, considered 
_as its exclusive scope the. safe- 
; guarding of liberty by law, Leo 
XIII admonished it that it had 


_ also the duty to interest itself 


_in social welfare, taking care of 
the entire people and of all its 
members, especially the weak and 
the dispossessed, through a gen- 
,erous social program and the cre- 
ation of a labor code. 

His call evoked a powerful re- 
_sponse; and it is a clear duty of 
justice to recognize the progress 
which has been achieved in the 
lot of workers through the pains 
_ taken by civil authorities in many 
lands. Hence was {ft well said that 
‘the Rerum Novarum became the 
Magna Charta of Christian social 
| endeavor. 

Meanwhile, there was passing 
‘a half-century which has left 
deep furrows and grievous dis- 
| turbance in the domain of nations 
| and society. The questions which 
sdcial and especially economic 

changes and upheavals offered 
for moral consideration after the 
Rerum Novarum have been treat- 
ed with penetrating acumen by 
our immediate predecessor in the 
‘encyclical Quadragesimo Anno. 
| The ten years that have followed 
it have been no less fraught with 
surprises in social and economic 
life than the years before it and 
have finally poured their dark 
‘and turbulent waters into the 
|sea of war whose unforeseen cur- 
rents may affect our economy 
and society. 


Difficult to Trace Future 


What problems and what par- 
ticular undertakings, some per- 
haps entirely novel, our social life 
will present to the care of the 
Church at ‘the end of this con- 
flict. which sets so many peoples 
against one another, it is difficult 
at the moment to trace or fore- 
see. 

| If, however, the future has its 
| roots in the past, if the experi- 


| ence of recent years is to be our 


| 


guide for the future, we feel we 
may avail ourselves of this com- 
memoration give some further 
directive moral principles on 
three fundamental values of s0- 
cial and economic life; and we 
shall de this animated by the 


very spirit of Leo XIII and un- 
folding his. views which were 
more than prophetic, presaging 
the social revolution of the day. 
These three fundamental values, 
which are closely connected one 
with the other, mutually com-. 
and dependent, are: 
he use of material goods, labor 
and the family. 


Pure Use or MareriaL Goops 


The encyclical Rerum Novarum 
expounds, on the question of prop- 
erty and man’s sustenance, prin- 
ciples which have lost nothing of 
their inherent vigor with the 

| passage of time, and today, fifty 
years after, strike their roots 
|deeper and retain their innate 
| vitality. 
In our encyclical Sertum Lae- 


| tiae, directed to the Bishops of 


)the United States of America, we 
called the attention of all to the 
basic idea of these principles, 
which consists, as we said, in the 
assertion of the unquestionable 
need “‘that the goods which were 
created by God for all men should 
flow equally to all according to 
‘the principles of justice and 
‘charity.’’ 
Every man as a living being 
gifted with reason has in fact 
jfrom nature the fundamental 
right to make use of the material 
goods of the earth while it is left 
to the will of man and to the 
juridical statutes of nations to 
regulate in greater detail the 
actuation of this right. 

This individual right cannot in 
any way be suppressed, even by 
other clear and undisputed rights 


| over material goods. 


Demands Private Property 


Undoubtedly the natural order 
deriving from God demands also 
private property and the free re- 
ciprocal commerce of goods by in- 
terchange and gift as well as the 
functioning of the State as a con- 
trol over both these institutions. 
But all this remains subordinated , 
to the natural scope of material 
goods and cannot emancipate it- 
self from the first and fundamen- © 
tal right which concedes their 
use to all men; but it should rath- 
er serve to make possible the 
actuation of this right in conform- 
ity with its scope. d, 

Only thus can we and must we 
insure that private property and 
the use of material goods bring 
to society peace and prosperity 
and long life, that they no longer 


set up precarious conditions 
which will give risé to struggles 
and jealousies and which are left 
to the mercy and the blind inter- 
play of force and weakness. 

The native right to the use of — 
material goods, intimately linked 
as it is to the dignity and other 
rights of the human person to- 
gether with the statutes men- 
tioned above, provides man with 
a secure material ,basis of the 
highest import on which. to rige 
to the fulfillment with reasonable 
moral duties.

right will insure the

BERLIN \—A Gere 


man high command communique: 


The mopping up of remaining 
British and Greek troops in the 
southern part of the island of 
Crete is progressing. 

Contact with Italian troops 
driving forward from the east was 
established yesterday near Iera- 
petra. Up to the present about 
10,000 British and Greeks have 
been taken prisoner. 

South of Crete, German fighting 

‘planes attacked light British sea 
forces, damaged: one destroyer 
seriously with bomb hits and shot 
idown four fighter planes of the 
‘Hurricane, type without loss to 
themselves. 
- In the same area of the sea, 
the German air force sank a mer- 
chant ship of 3,000 tons and a 
coastal ship loaded with muni- 
tions, and seriously damaged a 
smaller troop transport. 

Planes assigned to armed recon- 
naissance sank a British subma- 
rine in the Atlantic yesterday. 

Fighting squadrons bombed im- 
sortant war plants on the western 

and southern coasts of England 
ast night. Large fires were 
started, especially in the harbor: 
of the Mersey. 

East of Peterhead a large mer- 
thant ship was seriously damaged. 
.n May 30 fighting planes de- 
troyed a patrol boat in the har- 
vor at Fraserburgh. 

There were no enemy attacks 
wer Germany either by day or 
hight.

[By the Associated Press] 
London, June 2—Quick British mili- 
tary action against French-mandated 
Syria, where sea-borne German troops 
already are reported landing, was ex- 
pected by authoritative obseryers to-' 
night. 
The conviction was expressed in 
informed quarters that imperial forces 
in the Middle East were poised to 
strike at Syria to prevent the Nazis 
|from getting a foothold there and also 
to bolster Turkey, Britain’s non-bel- 
ligerent ally. 
With Britons conceding the Mediter- 
ranean to be virtually an Axis lake 
jand the dangerous turn of events in 
Syria, British authorities on Cyprus, 
island bastion standing between Axis- 
conquered Crete and Syria, began 
preparing for an anticipated German 
air assault. 
Doubt Turkey Will Yield 
Authoritative 7 nts expressed 
|confidence that Turkey would never 
yield to Axis threats or promises. But: 
‘whatever the Ankara Government 
does, they said, Britain was determined

and the Middle East oil fields, would!

tended.

bring extreme pressure on Turkey

shave isolated the Dardanelles from any 
hope of assistance by the royal navy.|

Nazis Tried to Trick Him. 


“Neal’s gun was still firing on 
May 27, the day the last of us 
cleared out of Canea. I don’t 
know whether he got away alive. 

“Each time the group of Nazi 
dive-bombers came over, Neal 
would let all but the last plane 
pass, Then he would concentrate 
upon it. He got nine within two 
days and at least twenty within 
the fortnight.” 

The Nazis soon caught on to 
the itactic, Mohoney continued, 

S0 they decided to get him by 


the Nazi-conquered.island. | 


trickery.”

mation, and the three trailing 
planes then plastered his gun} 
position repeatedly with stic 
of heavy bombs. 


Gasoline Dumps Fired.

Neal’s gun kept firing while 
bombs burst about him, some 
firing the gasoline dumps and 
ammunition depots nearly with 
terrific explosions.

couragement to his companions. 

“Then he countered the Nazi- 
counter-tactic. He attached the 
gun to a light lorry, and imme- 
diately after firing at the last 
plane of a formation, Neal and 
other marines hopped onto the 
lorry and moved the guns to an- 
other position. ;

[By the Associated Press] 


Berlin, J 2—Adolf Hitler and Be- 
nito Mussclini discussed the “political 
situation” at Brenner Pass today only 
a few hours after the German high 
command had put its seal on an an- 
nouncement of the successful conclu- 
sion of the battle for Crete. 

A number of fateful possibilities re- 
garding Africa and the Mediterranean 
area lay before the two rulers, but the 
announcement of their meeting men- 
tioned none of them. 

The Fiihrer and the Duce, with their 
respective Foreign Ministers, Joachim 
von Ribbentrop and (-:int Galeazzo 
Ciano beside them, fyIked for five 


ship,” a communique stated, adding, 
that the talk show. “complete agree- 
ment of views. 
Next M ‘e Yiscussed 
In the immediate past #ere the suc- 


| Discussion thr ~»x* step would seem 
to have betn calied Jer and, to follow 
the stated p.an of attacking the British 
'wherever they are found, this would 
open an expansive vista—the Mediter- 
ranean, the waters of the Red seal} 


desert sands. 


agenda of the meeting. DNB in al 
guarded dispatch méntioned “the 
successful and victory-erowned mili- 
tary operations of thé? Axis powers 
in the lediterranean and ‘North Af- 
rica” as GQminating the outer aspects 
of the meeting and said the end 
of operations in Crete was a sub- 
ject of discussion. 

Although Hitler-Mussolini meetings 
usually are discussed only in vague 


terms, recent history shows that they 
usually are accompanied or followed 
by decisive action. 
It seemed likely to observers here 
that the entire strategic picture in 
the Mediterranean, Africa and the 
| Near East was reviewed and decisions 
for the future made, The press to- 
day already was taking the occupation 
of Crete into calculation as a spring- 
board to Suez or Alexandria. 
Drive ‘Across Libya 
The discussions undoubtedly would 
include the German-Italian drive 
across Libya to the Egyptian frontier. 
the defeat of the Italian army in 
Ethiovia; Gibraltar, Cyprus, Suez and 
the British position in Egypt and the 
changed situation in Iraq. | 
But whatever decisions were 
reached are the secrets of the Axis 
leaders and their high commands. 
The announcement of the German 
high command that Crete finally had 
been wrested from the British’ and

[By the Associated Press] 

Hyde Park, N. Y., June 2—President 
Roosevelt appeared moving tonight in 
the dir of new and possibly 
momentous decisions on stepping up 
aid to England. 

The completion of two legislative 
Steps to strengthen America’s arma- 
ment effort—the expansion of manda- 
tory priorities aud the opening of 


boats—was disclosed by the tempo-| 
rary White House. °

such dimensions as ta measure up to 
expectations raised by last Tuesday's 
fireside chat,- some members of the 
Presidential party here suggested.) 
seemed to hinge to a large extent on 
a conference in the capital tomorrow 
between Mr.-Roosevelt and John G. 
Winant, United States Ambassador 
to London, now ‘h-me to report.

The Douglas Aircraft Company, 
of Santa Monica, Calif., and the 
Vega Airplane Company (Lockheed 
subsidiary), of Burbank, Calif., will 
work with Boeing in an unpreced- 
ented production scheme. 

The company said each of the 
factories would turn out completely 
assembled four-engine bombers. | 
identical to the point of interchang-. 
ability cf parts, and that several 
other manufacturers would co-op- 
erate through subcontracts. 

The plan was worked out at the 


- request of the War Department to 


help meet the demand for four- 
engined, long-range bombers in vast 
numbers, the announcement said. 

The Boeing Company also an- 
nounced immediate construction of 
a large addition to its plant No, 2 
Stearman division in Wichita, Kan.., 
as another part of its bomber pro- 
duction program. 

The enlargement will quadruple, 
the present 384,000 square feet -f 
floor space.

Three Objectives Seen. 


Mr. Jouett said American air 
research engineers were working 
toward: 

Making airplanes faster by 
shooting engine exhaust gases 
through jets ta, obtain a “rocket 
propulsion” effect. 

Increasing propeller effeciency 
by installing counter-rotating 
propellers which would obviate 
the engine torque effect common 
to all planes. Experiments have 
been in progress at the Army Air 
Corps laboratory at Wright Field, 
Dayton, in that field. © 

Equipping American pursuit 
planes with five times as many 
cannon as they now carry. One 
standard pursuit plane manu- 
factured in the United States car- 
ries a single cannon, another 
manufactured for the ‘British, 
two. 

Mr. Jouett asserted that the 
Germans, now stopping produc- 
tion to retool on new models of 
bombers and fighters, “are no- 
where near up to their protential 
production capacity.” 

Of the aircraft factory labor 
situation, he forecast the employ- 
ment of a half-million persons in 
the next few months, adding,

directed alternately in Washington

in Montreal Sunday 
Lol. Wedgwood, in an interview 
at the Hotel Pennsylvania, said that 
his proposed union of democratic 
nations should begin with Anglo- 
American unity. He said that “the 
safety for democracies is in 
union,” and that every democratic’ 
country should be ‘admitted. 
Discussing American aid to Great 
. ritain, Col. Wedgwood said, “I 
have a feeling it is our job to hold 
tlic fortress and the job of the United 
States to tell us what to do and give 
us all material aid.” He said there 
could be no negotiated peace, “as it 
y would give Hitler time to build 
a gigantic naval fleet and air force.”

[By the Associated Press] 
Lisbon, June 2--Weary and be- 
draggled, 119 American survivors of 
the Egyptian liner Zamzam, sunk by a 
German raid 1 17, arrived at 
suburban Sintra today. 


| Most planned to await passage to 


the United States, which may take a 
few weeks, while some of the 107 mis- 
sionaries and their families hoped to 
‘continue to Africa. 

After coffee and baths many went 
to sleep since they had been sitting up 
for forty-eight hours in day coaches 
from the Spanish border. All except 
two slightly feverish children were in 
good health.

Middle East command com- 
munique: 

LIBYA: At Tobruk and in the 
Salum (Sollum) area our troops 
have again been active. 


ETHIOPIA: In the lakes area, 
operations are continuing. 


. TRAK: In Bagdad the situation is 
rapidly returning to normal, and at 
Basra all is calm. 

A Royal Air Force communique: 


MEDITERRANEAN: Large for- 
mations of fighter aircraft of the 
R. A. F. and S.A. A. F., continued 
their protective pauuis thruugh- 
out yesterday over His Majesty's 
ships and merchant vessels en- 
gaged in evacuation of our troops 
from Crete. 


Enemy aircraft were repeatedly 
intercepted and many were at- 
tacked. 

Several others, including four 
S-79’s and three JU-88’s were com- 
pelled to retire before they had 
time to make an attack. 

On the previous day a CANT- 
1007 and an ME-110 were destroyed 
by our patrols while on similar 


_ duty. These are in addition to seven 


enemy aircraft claimed in yester- 


| day’s communique. 


CRETE: During the night of 


| May 31-June 1 our heavy bombers 


attacked airdromes at Malemi 
(Meleme) and Candia (Heraclion) 
on the Island of Crete, several fires 
were started and four enemy air- 
craft were destroyed on the ground 
at Malemi. A number of others are 
believed to have been destroyed at 
Almyro. 

In addition to these protective 
and bombing activities which have 
taken place throughout the whole 
period of the evacuation, a num- 
ber of our bombers have been en- 
gaged in dropping medical sup- 
plies and food to isolated bodies of 
our troops in Crete. 


ETHIOPIA: In East Africa air- 
craft of the S. A. A. F. success- 
fully bombed and machine-gunned 
enemy positions and motor trans- 
port in the Jimma area, obtaining 
direct hits on buildings and ve- 
hicles, 

R. A. F. aircraft bombed enemy 
troop concentrations at Deberak 
and aircraft of a “Free French” 
unit bombed and machine-gunned 
enemy motor transport 
Chelga and Azozo. 

LIBYA: During the night of 
May $1-June 1 our heavy bombers 
again attacked the harbor at Ben- 

‘Zasi and started fires which were 


visible forty miles away, An S. A. 


A. F. fighter shot down a German 
Henschel aircraft near Tobruk 
yesterday and machine-gunned 
military buildings at Menastir. 


SYRIA: In Syria our bombers 
raided Aleppo Airdrome and de- 
stroyed a large twin-engined en- 
emy aircraft by a direct hit and 
damaged. several others. 

From all these operations one of 
our aircraft is missing. 


LONDON, June 2 (®).—A Minis- 
tries of Air and Home Security 
communique: 

There was considerable enemy 
activity over this country last night, 
mostly in the northwest of Eng- 
land, where there were several in- 
cidents and a sharp attack devel- 
oped in one district. — 


Several fires were started, some 
damage was done, and there was a 
number of casualties including 
some people killed. 


Bombs were drggped at several 
in : s elsewhere

chub few casualties are re-

An evening communique of the 
Air and Home Security Ministries: 


During the day (Monday) there 
has been some slight enemy activ- 
ity off our coasts. 

Single enemy aircraft dropped 
bombs at a point in the north- 
east, but only minor damage was 
done. 

No casualties have been reported. 


An Air Ministry communique: 


An aircraft of the coastal com- 
mand while on patrol yesterday en- 
countered an enemy fighter off the 
west coast of France and shot it 
down into the sea, 

An aircraft of the same com- 
mand is missing from patrol yes- 
‘terday. 

Two enemy fighters are now 
known to have been destroyed dur- 
ing last night’s raids on this 
country.

LONDON, June 2 (®).—A com- 
munique of the Netherlands Royal 
Navy issued through the British 
Admiralty: ~ 


The French ship Winnipeg, 8,379 
tons, has been intercepted by the 
Royal Netherlands warship Van- 
kinsbergen (Comadr. J. L. K. Hoeke) 
in West Indian waters. 

The Winnipeg had sailed from 
Marseilles and had called at Dakar. 
When intercepted she had on 
board over 200 Germans bound for 
Martinique. The reason for the 


presence of these Germans is not | 
at all clear and is now undergoing | 


examination. 


rman 


high command communique: 


The battle for Crete is ended. The 


entire island is free of the enemy. 
German troops yesterday oc- 
cupied the last stronghold of the 


defeated British, the harbor of 


Sphakion. 


Mountain troops broke the last 
British resistance in the mountain 
country north of Sphakion and | 


brought in 3,000 more prisoners. 


The German Air Force effectively | 
supported these final mopping-up | 


actions. 
In waters between Crete and 
Alexandria German fighting 


planes destroyed, with three full | 


hits, a destroyer in a fleet unit. 


In North Africa there was artil- | 


lery and reconnaissance activity 
near Tobruk. 
German and Italian planes sank 


five small transport ships in the | 


harbor of Tobruk and damaged 
enemy anti-aircraft batteries ef- 
fectively. 


Strong air forces last ‘night 


bombed the British supply center | 


of Manchester with numerous ex- 
plosive and incehdiary bombs. 
Heavy explosions and extensive 
fires caused new and great de- 
struction among factories and 
warehouses. 

Further air attacks directed 
against harbors on the th and 
southwest coasts of England. 

Southeast of Aberdeen and north 
of Scotland the Luftwaffe destroyed 
two enemy merchant ships totaling 
6,000 tons and badly damaged an- 
other merchant ship. 

There was no enemy 


action over 


German soil either by day or night.

In North Africa, Italian and

ships in the harbor and installa-

small steamers were sunk, and 
anti-aircraft batteries were square- 
ly hit. A gasoline depot was set 
afire. 

On the night of May 31 British 
planes bombed Bengasi. An enemy 


plane was shot down by anti-air- 


craft defense. The crew of five | 
persons was captured.

with German units, have reached | 
fixed objectives.

[By the Associated Press] 

Valletta, Malta, June 3—A German Junkers troop-carrying 
plane, believed by Bry urces to be empty of soldiers, was 
shot down in irae ghter planes today during the sec- 
ond of avo WO raids on this British Mediterranean strong- 
hold. 


The communique said: | 
“Last night enemy aircraft which approached the island 


| were caught by searchlights and repulsed by anti-aircraft fire. 


“This afternoon there were two attempted raids. The first 


was repulsed by defense batteries and during the second our 
fighters shot down into the sea a Junkers 52. 


21 eye 
“During May, twenty-one civilians were killed by enemy 


action.” 


The communique left unanswered how many planes were 


_in the raids and whether the Germans or Italians actually had 


attempted a landing; if other big Junkers were in the attack- 
ing force. 
This report iced speculation as to whether the Axis 
meant to invade Malta as a stepping stone to North Africa. 
Malta underwent its first air raid June 11, 1940, the day 
after Italy entered the war.

tobacco from occupied China alone,

Defense Spending 
= ederal Debt Now $47,720,765,7 +} 
‘Waskineton, June 3 (A. P.).—Defense spending jumped to a 


new peacét igh of $27,000,000 a day last month and to 
Ty’ sat@"the.yignth continued the steady rise in 


The Treas 


defense spending which bég’'an on July 1 last. July expenditures 
were $177,325,479, or about a fifth of the May total. . 

For eleven months, defense costs aggregated $5,240,420,822, 
nearly half the total Federal costs of $11,229,288,081. The pro- 
portion has been increasing, and lately defense costs have been 
approximately two-thirds of all Federal expenditures. 

Next year the Budget Bureau has estimated defense costs 
will total $15,500,000,000 and the total budget $22,169,000,000. 

Although revenues of $6,331,203,008 in the last eleven months 
were far ahead of last year, the tax gain did not equal the rise 
in spending, and the eleven months’ deficit was $4,898,085,073, 
compared with $3,455,692,542 a year ago. Partly due to the new 
‘@efensé savings campaign, the Federal debt has been rising 
daily to new records and now is $47,720,765,728—nearly $5,000,- 
000,000 higher than a year ago.

Capture Ethiopian Village 
Ethiopian troops led by British offi- 

cefs ‘have captured the village of 
Debarech for the third time after 
extremely bitter fighting, British mili- 
headquarters announced today, 
listing taken thus far 
in the drive to stamp out the last . 
Italian resistance in the south central 
lakes district. 

Debarech twice was retaken by the 
dogged Italians before the Ethiopians 
and British secured it firmly, aided 
in part by British pressure from the 
south in an advance which is still con- 
tinuing.

Gover subsidizes 
French Film Industry 


State Will Advance 65%. of 
projected Pictures’ Cost 


VI 
government announced today a plan 
which would virtually subsidize the 
French motion-picture industry in 
competition with foreign films. 

The government recently issued a 
decree banning the showing of any 
films which were exhibited in 
France before October, 1937, with 
the exception of a few of unusual 
merit or educational interesi. Both 
measures were undertaken to build 
up the French film industry, which 
has been badly hit by the war, par- 
ticularly because of a shortage of 
raw film. 

The new law authorizes the state 


stone of the new State, which he 


to advance up to 65 per cent of 
the total cost of the projected film, 
‘the remainder to be assumed by the 
producer. ‘The statute increases 
state control of French film produc- 
tion, which already was under su- 
pervision, as actors can play only

DNB, the official German news

High Command announcement 
that more Britons and 
4,000 Greeks had been captured 
a figure which author- 
ized sources said brought the to- 
tal of Crete captives to 25,000. 

DNB said the death list did not

it said would be many times as 
heavy as those on land.

ewspaper, the Voelkischer Heo-

between Hitler and Premier Mus-.

Britain’s troubles in Iraq were all 
settled. 
Prepared For Syria 

An said that 
the British precautions in Syria were 
made possible by the settlement over 
the week end of the undeclared war 
with Iraq. This, he said, “has enabled 
us to prepare for whatever may blow 
up in Syria.” He added: 

“It would be wrong to assume that 
all activities and preparations in that 
part of the world are French and 
German.” 

British forces have occupied Mosul, 
the oil center of northern Iraq, and 
it was reported that the Mosul pipe 
line to Haifa, Palestine, was in work- 
ing order despite the recent presence 
of German airmen in Mosul. .

Washington, June 4 (P)—The United 
States registered formal indignation 
today over the invasion of Yugoslavia, 
It was contained in a note from 
Sumner Welles, Under Secretary of 
State, to stantin Fotitch, the Yugo- 
slav Minister in United States. 
The note was in reply to notes re- 
ceived from the Yugoslav Government 
protesting the creation of a so-called 
“independent state.of Croatia.” Welles 
acknowledged these notes and re- 
iterated “the indignation of this Gov- 
ernment and the American people at 
the invasion and mutilation of Yugo-| 


the tripartite pact.” The Welles note 
emphasized the continued recognition 
by the United States of the Yugoslav 
Government of young King Peter, now

may serve beer and wine only with

liquor may be sold anywhere in the 
Province on Sundays and beer and

BOR 8) WASHINGTON, DECLARED TONIGHT THERE WOULD BE NOTHING TO GAIN BY. 


WAR BETWEEN JAPAN AND THE UNITED STATES AND STRESSED THE IMPORTANCE OF 


KEEPING THE PRESENT CONFLICT OUT OF THE PACIFIC,

THAT THE CONFLAGRATION NOW RAGING ELSEWHERE MUST NOT SPREAD TO THAT 
OCEAN, IT IS CLEAR HAVE NOTHING TO GAIN AND 
EVERYTHING TO LOSE BY ARMED CONFLICT. BETWEEN AMERICA AND JAPAN THE 
WAY OF PEACE IS THE ONLY WAY, _

IS NO REASON TO LOSE FAITH IN OUR ABILITY TO WORK OUT A PEACEFUL

To the Congress of the United 
States: 


I recommend authorization of 
construction of the St. Lawrence 
seaway and power project, pursu- 
ant to the agreement of March 19, 
1941, with Canada, as an integral 
part of the joint defense of the 
Nerth American Continent. 


Production and more production 
is the keynote of our all-out race 


for national defense. Electric | 
power and transportation are lim- 
ting factors in the production of 
planes, guns, tanks and ships. 


| The enemies of democracy are | 
Meveloping hydro-electric resource 
mgr every waterway from Norway 

the Dardanelles. Are we to allow 
this continent to be out-matched 
because short-sighted interests op- 
[pose the development of one of our 
‘greatest resources? 


' Your action on this project will 
either make available or withhold 
2,200,000 horsepower of low-cost 
electric power for the joint defense 
of North America. Your action on 
this project will either open or |

ten billion kilowatt hours a year.

I should like to agree with the ™aterials, including large quantities of 
, people who say that the country’s aluminum now held by private indus- | 


_ danger will be over sooner than 
, that. But the course of world 
events gives no such assurance; 
and we have no right to take 
«chances with the national safety. 
I know of no single project of 
this nature more important to 
this country’s future in peace or 
war. Its authorization will demon- 
Strate to the enemies of democ- 
racy that, however long the effort, 
we intend to outstrip them in 
the race of production. In the 
modern world that race deter- 
mines the rise and fall of nations. 


I hope that authorization will 
not be delayed.

More Than 200 | 

rLasab] ca, French Morocco, June 4 
Datayed highly placed in- 
formant At bat says the German 
armistice commission in Morocco con- 
sists of about 125 men, including 
officers, and that it never has num- 
bered more than 200. 

This informant made the statement, 
as he said, to refute “the fantastic 
rumors started periodically by badly 
intentioned persons insinuating the 
existence in this country of German 
air and naval bases.” 

Members of the commission are 
comfortably installed in an aristocratic 
section of Casablanca where phono- 
graph music is one of their diversions. 
Their neighbors say, however, that 
the choice of music is limited largely 


to English records, including “Lam- 
beth Walk” and “We'll Hang Out the

Geography. Halifax has one of 
the finest natural harbors in the 
world. Further inland, connected! . 
with the outer harbor by a deep 
narrows, lies a huge anchorage 
basin, where scores of ships may 
lie, awaiting convoy. The port is 


ice-free all winter. 


Geography also made Nova 
Scotia, and more particularly 


Halifax harbor, a’British perch to 
flank the French in North Amer- 
ica in “the eighteenth century. 
Colony in 
1749, sent it to the harbor of 


‘Parliament subsidized 


‘bucto in Acadia! That colony 
came Halifax. 

There followéd the French and 
Indian wars, when British Navy 
paymasters brought prosperity to 
Halifax. Then the American rev- 
jOlution. Halifax remained loyal 
to the end, supplied blockading 
fleets and privateers for the Brit- 
ish. And made money. 

During the Napoleonic wars 
the Royal Navy kept big fleets in 
the harbor. Wolfe planned the 
capture of Quebec here. And it 
was here during the war of 1812 
that the world’s first modern con- 
voys were made up and escorted 


T 


The story was the same in the 
Crimean and Boer wars. The big 
push of prosperity came in 1914, 
It was the same business of sup- 
plying rendezvous for convoys, a 
North Atlantic base for the Brit- 


of France- 
furnished here, too. Halifax was 
in the money again. 

Through it all, Haligonians have! 
not rested content to reap the. 
profit of war. They’ve also taken 
the risks» You can name hardly 
a battle fought by British forces 
janywhere in the world, without 
running into a Haligonian hero.,

WITH VICHY 
DAMASCUS, SYRIA, JUNE 4-(AP)-THE COMMANDER IN CHIEF OF 


FRENCH EMPIRE FORCES IN S$ » GENERAL HENRI DENTZ, BAS ORDERED HIS 


MEN TO THEIR POSTS FOR AN UNYIELDING DEFENSE OF THESE MANDATED LANDS, 
MYPOTKETICAL VICTORY WHICH IN COMING YEARS WOULD RESTORE GRANDEUR TO 


CALTIONING THEM AGAINST BRITISH PROMISES AND DENYING THAT MILITARY 


CONTROL IS BEING SURRENDERED TO GERMANY. 


“THE COMMAND IS CLEAR,” ME SAID IN A RADIO ADDRESS LAST NIGHT TO 
THE ARMIES OF THE LEVANT. “DEFEND OUR POSSESSIONS AND DEFEND THEM 
WITH YOUR OWN FORCES.” | 

ME SAID THE PEOPLES OF THE LEVANT WANT “NO OTHER EMANICPATORS BUT 
YOU. 

"XXX I SIMPLY ASKYOU---THE MARSHAL (PETAIN) DEMANDS THIS---TO MAIN- 
TAIN OUR POSSESSION OF THE TERRITORIES BELONGING TO US OR ENTRUSTED TO 
OUR PROTECTION. NOTHING MORE, NOTHING LESS. 

“THERE IS NOTHING IN THIS CONTRARY TO TYE HONOR AND INTERESTS OF 
FRANCE. YOU HAVE HEARD ME. YCU HAVE UNDERSTOOD ME. I SAY NO MORE. 

REFERRING TO THE RECENT PASSAGE OF GERMAN WARPLANES TO IRAQ, VIA 
SYRIAN AIR BASES WHICH ARE GERMANY*S TO CONTROL UNDER THE ARMISTICE | 
TERMS, KE SAID THAT THE SAME PLANES NOW WERE RETURNING. 


REGARDING REPORTS ABROAD THAT FRANCE WAS ABANDONING SYRIA TO GERMAN 
MILITARY CONTROL, THAT GERMAN TROOPS MAD BEEN LANDED AT LATAKIA AND 
THAT 500 GERMANS MAD ARRIVED AT BEIRUT ON A FRENCH HOSPITAL SHIP, 
DENTZ SAID ALL THESE STORIES WERE UNFOUNDED. — 


“THERE ARE AND MAVE NEVER SEEN GERMAN TROOPS IN SYRIA AND LEBANON," 


SAID. 


ELABORATING ON VICHY*S COLLABORATION POLICY, THE GENERAL WARNED 
AGAINST SEDUCTIVE PROMISES --- A REFERENCE TO BRITISH D “FREE FRENCH" 
AGITATION---AND SAID “THEY DAZZLE YOU WITH THE ALLURING IMAGE OF 


FRANCE. 
“IT IS CERTAIN, HOWEVER, THAT IF NOTHING IS UNDERTAKEN NOW TO 


ASSURE TME SUBSTANCE OF FRANCE, NO FRENCHMEN WILL REMAIN IN FRANCE 


BY THEN.” JUN 1941

On all fronts the situation re-

A Royal Air Force communique: 

Heavy bombers of the R, A. F. 
attacked the airdrome at Katta- 
via, on the Island of Rhodes, dur- 
ing the night of June 4-5. Bombs 
straddled runways and fell among 
hangars and. airdrome buildings, 
causing a number of violent ex- 
plosions which were followed by 
fires. 

Our aircraft bombed military 
buildings at Derna and the harbor 
at Bengasi, damaging the ceniral 
mole. 

R. A. F. bombers attacked Ital- 
jan aircraft on the airdrome at 
Aleppo yesterday and destroyed 
one with a direct hit. Bombs were 
also observed to burst on hangars 
and tarmac (runways). Columns 
of smoke rose from the tarmac 
after the attack. 

Bombing and machine-gun at- 
tacks were carried out by aircraft 
of the R. A. F. and the South

“Some say I have changed my posi- 
tion because, whereas I criticized Mr. 
Roosevelt very severely during the 
campaign, I have done my’ best since 
then to support him in each of his 
policies as I believe to be best for this 
country. That is a catch argument. 

Assails Partisan Motives 

“Of course I criticized Mr. Roosevelt, 

and I disagree with him right now on, 


many questions of domestic policy, on, 


the administration of many defense 
efforts. 

“But the American people expressed 
their will in a free election. They 
chose Franklin D. In this 
hour of great national peril it would 
be downright folly, if not worse, for 
any man to spend his time in criti- 
cizing and obstructing the efforts of 
the Administration, for. the sake of 
mere partisan advantage or for the 
vanity of a seeming consistency.

defer the induction into the armed 
forces of selective service registrants 
who have reached their twenty-eighth 
birthday was approved today by the 
Senate Military Affairs Committee. 

Scheduled for consideration in the 
Senate next Tuesday, the measure 
would permit the President to narrow 
the service age limits so that only men 
from 21 through 27 years old would 
be called up, instead of the present 
bracket of 21 through 35. 

Adopts Move 5-3 

Sponsors said the measure would 
not affect men 28 years old or older 
who already have been inducted into 
the armed forces Nor, they added, 
was it to be considered a permanent 
exemption for other men in this age 
bracket, since they would ‘be liable

group.

(By the Associated Press) 

Atlanta, June 6—Electric power con- 

-defense industries in 
|the Southeast will be rationed after 
6 A. M., June 16, Georgia Power Com- 
|pany officials disclosed today. 
|. The rationing systerh will be placed 
in effect by the Tennessee Valley Au- 
thority and Commonwealth and South- 


jern subsidiaries, including the Georgia 
utility, the Alabama Power Company, 


S- 


|which. navy officials believe will be. 
|ready for service by next September, 
a full year ahead of its scheduled com- | 


Members of Congress attended the, 


will preside at the laying of the keel) | 


‘the South Carolina Power Company, 
the Mississippi Power Company and . 
the Gulf Power Company, 


Low Water Declared Cause 


Power company spokesmen attrib- 
‘uted the move to falling lake and 
river levels with a resultant slowing 
of turbine movement. 

Originally scheduled for June 9, 
action was postponed to permit com- 
panies and the TVA to work out de- 
tails for the allotment of power to 
industry and to lessen confusion 


jamong consumers, the power company 


said, 

J. A. Krug, chief TVA power en. 
Knoxville that a pro- 
gram hed been worked out with 
municipalities and cooperatives dis- 
tributing TVA power to conserve 
power. Details of the program prob- 
ably will be made public tomorrow, he 
Said. ‘ 

Decided At Closed Session 


The rationing system was decided 
upon at a closed session here last 
Wednesday by chairman’ 
»of the Federal Power Commission; rep- 
resentatives of the Commonwealth and 
Southern subsidiaries, the TVA, the 
Security Exchange Commission, the 
army and the navy. At that time Old: 
hinted that rationing was in the offing 

Previously the companies appealec 
to all consumers for an overall twenty 
per cent. reduction in the use of elec- 
. tricity. Southeastern cities almost im- 
| mediately complied. Ornamental light- 
ing was eliminated and whiteway: 
darkened. 


Began In December 
Actually the drought, which now has 
a stranglehold on the Southeast, began 
in December, 1939, although the situa- 
tion is greatly accentuated by the fact 
that no general rains have fallen in 
the region for the last forty-two days, 

Clear skies again prevailed over the 
area today and, the Weather Bureau 
said, no rains will fall before Sun- 
day or Monday. These rains probably 
will be in the form of showers and 
precipitation will be irregular, Cror 
losses are gontinuing to mount.

“FORTY MILLION WOMEN INVITED TO WORK TOWARD NATIONAL DEFENSE, 


WOMEN'S ORGANIZATIONS 


LINE UP TO WORK OU 
PROGRAM OF ACTION (900) 


JUN 7 = 1941 


BY RUTH COWAN 

(ADVANCE) WASHINGTON, JUNE $--(THE SPECIAL NEWS SERVICE)--BRIMMING 
WITH ENERGY AND PURPOSE, MRS.JOHN L.WHITEHURST, PRESIDENT OF 
THE GENERAL FEDERATION OF WOMEN'S CLUBS, IS OUT TO LINE UP UNCLE 
SAM'S DAUTHERS, FROM BABY SISTER TO GREAT-GRANDMA, TO GIVE 
A HELPING HAND IN NATIONAL DEFENSE, 

CONVINCED THAT WOMEN WANT TO DO THEIR PART AND ARE IMPATIENT TO 
GET GOING, MRSsWHITEHURST IS TAKING THE INITIATIVE IN PITCHING 
THE BALL TOWARD A GOAL, SHE SAID, OF AT LEAST 40,000,000 GIRLS 


AND WOMEN AT WORK STRENGTHENING THE NATION'S RAMPARTS. 


AS THE FIRST STEP SHE INVITED THE PRESIDENTS OF NEARLY 75 NATIONAL 


WOMEN'S ORGANIZATIONS TO MEET IN NEW YORK NEXT FRIDAY“'TO WORK OUT 
A PROGRAM OF VOLUNTARY -ACTIVITIES TO BE CO-ORDINATED WITH THOSE OF 
THE GOVERNMENT'S CIVILIAN.DEFENSE COMMITTEE, 


MRS «WHITEHURST, WHOSE HOME IS IN BALTIMORE, ALREADY HAS 


SOME EXPERIENCE ALONG THE LINE SHE IS TACKLING. APPOINTED BY GOVERNOR 


HERBERT R,O*CONOR, SHE IS THE ONLY WOMAN ON 1.1/E MARYLAND COUNCIL 


QF DEFENSE AND RESOURCES, AND THE STATE LEGISLATURE AUTHORIZED 


HER SELECTION AS CO-ORDINATOR OF WOMEN'S DEFENSE ACTIVITIES, 

IMPRESSED WITH THE POTENTIAL VALUE OF WORK WOMEN CAN DO, SHE 
DETERMINED WHEN ELECTED HEAD OF THE WORLD'S LARGEST ORGANIZATION 
OF WOMEN THREE WEEKS AGO AT THE GENERAL FEDERATION'S CONVENTION IN 
ATLANTIC CITY TO CALL UPON ITS 2,000,000 MEMBERS TO LEAD THE WAY 
IN MOBILIZATION OF THE NATION'S WOMAN-POWER. JUN 7a] 

SHE ASKED HER FELLOW-CLUB MEMBERS TO FOLLOW HER IN lasact 
TO PRESERVE OUR REPRESENTATIVE FORM OF GOVERNMENT, TO COMBAT FIFTH 


COLUMNISTS AND TO PREPARE OUR COUNTRY AND OUR PEOPLE TO DEFEND THEM- 


SELVES." 


THE RESPONSE WAS A THOUSAND LETTERS AND TELEGRAMS WITHIN A FEW 


DAYS FROM WOMEN--"AND SOME MENJ."--ASKING HOW THEY COULD HELP, 


SHE RELATED, SHE HAD FIVE LETTERS OF OPPOSITION, AND THEIR 


OBJECTION WAS TO HELPING BRITAIN, SHE SAID, ONE OF THESE LETTERS 


HER A "NURDERESS." 


BUT SUCH NAME-CALLING DID NOT DAUNT THIS TALL, HANDSOME 
WOMAN WHO HAS MUCH PISE AND FORCE OF MANNER, TO THE GENERAL FEDERA- 


TION'S SEVEN DEPARTMENTS SHE ADDED A NEW ONE, NATIONAL DEFENSE, 


AND APPOINTED MRS,HAROLD V.MILLIGAN OF NEW YORK, CHAIRMAN,

SPECIAL NEWS SERVICE ADVANCE FOR AMS Or SATURDAY, JUNE 7, 


(ADVANCE) WASHINGTON--FIRST ADD WOMEN IN NATIONAL DEFENSE, BY COWAN 
XXX WOMEN'S ASSOCIATIONS, Jbiv 1941 

MRS .WHITEHURST SAID IT IS NOT THE PLAN TO SET UP A NEW OVER- 
ALL WOMEN'S DEFENSE ORGANIZATION BUT RATHER TO TIE TOGETHER THE 
DEFENSE ACTIVITIES OF EXISTING WOMEN'S CLUBS TO PREVENT OVER- 
LAPPING AND WASTED EFFORTS, 

ONE AIM IS TO MAKE MORE EASY THEIR CO-OPERATION WITH STATE 
AND LOCAL: DEFENSE COUNCILS WHEN SUCH ARE SET UP UNDER THE NEW CIVI- 
LIAN DEFENSE COMMITTEE, MRS.WHITEHURST SAID SHE WAS ASKING 
MAYOR F.H.LA GUARDIA OF NEW YORK, PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT'S 
APPOINTEE AS CIVILIAN DEFENSE DIRECTOR, TO ADDRESS THE MEETING, 

BUT ASIDE FROM LA GUARDIA, SHE SAID SHE DID NOT PLAN TO 
INVITE OTHER SPEAKERS, THE MEETING SHOULD BELONG To THE WOMEN 


FOR DISCUSSION AND PLANNING, SHE SAID. SHE BELIEVES THAT 


WOMEN WANT TO DEVELOP THEIR OWN LEADERSHIP AND PROGRAMS, 


MRS»WHITEHURST IS OPPOSED TO GOVERNMENT CONSCRIPTION OF WOMEN 
IN DEFENSE WORK, WOMEN CAN DO VOLUNTEER WORK AND STILL KEEP 
THEIR HOMES RUNNING, SHE SAID. “ON A VOLUNTEER BASIS THEY CAN 
SELECT THEIR OWN TIME TO DO DEFENSE WORK, BUT IF CONSCRIPTED, 
THEY WOULD HAVE TO WORK WHEN TOLD TO,” SHE SAID. 

AS PRESIDING OFFICER AT THE MEETING, MRS.WHITEHURST SAID 
SHE WOULD SUGGEST THAT THE VARIOUS WOMEN'S ORGAnIZATIONS IN EACH 
STATE DECIDE UPON A STATE CO-ORDINATOR AS THEIR REPRESENTATIVE, 
SHE MIGHT OR MIGHT NOT BE A FEDERATION CLNBWOMAN, MRS. 
WHITEHURST SAID. JUN 7- 1941 

LIKEWISE CO-ORDINATORS SHOULD BE SELECTED FOR EACH COUNTY, SHE 
SAID. THE STATE CO-ORDINATOR WOULD BE AID BY AN ADVISORY 
COMMITTEE COMPOSED OF REPRESENTATIVES SELECTED BY THE VARIOUS WOMEN'S 
ORGANIZATIGNS. 


IN WORKING OUT THE GENERAL FEDERATION'S DEPARTMENT OF NATIONAL 


DEFENSE PROGRAM, MRS.WHITEHURST HAS SET UP A NUMBER OF COMM- 


ITTEES, ONE WILL REGISTER THE CLUB MEMBERS VARIOUS SKILLS AND 
ABILITIES. 

THE AGRICULTURE COMMITTEE WILL PLAN THE TRAINING OF WOMEN IN 
SCIENTIFIC FARMING SO THAT THEY CAN TAKE THE PLACE OF MEN IF 
NEEDED, MRS,WHITEHURST SPOKE OF SEEKING AID FROM THE LAND
